A preview that declares its own colours wins: an ICC profile (the browser applies it, nothing is converted twice), or an EXIF ColorSpace in the JPEG (1 sRGB, 2 Adobe RGB, `0xFFFF` + `R03` Adobe RGB). `web/src/engine/meta/preview-space.ts`. | LightCraft `crates/raw/src/preview.rs` `embedded_preview_color_space` (lines 17–50) and `crates/codecs/src/jpeg.rs` `decode_with_fallback` (line 194); the JPEG's own EXIF read as darktable reads it (`src/common/exif.cc`, “Some files have the colorspace explicitly set”, lines 2758–2782) |
| LibRaw's EXIF | LibRaw puts its own EXIF block (make, exposure… but no ColorSpace) in front of a preview that has none (`dcraw_make_mem_thumb`, `src/postprocessing/mem_image.cpp` lines 81–112; `tiff_head` in `src/write/file_write.cpp`, LibRaw 0.22.1). EXIF without a ColorSpace therefore counts as saying nothing. LightCraft reads the raw bytes itself and doesn't need this. | Found by the e2e test |
| How | Such a preview gets an Adobe RGB (1998) ICC profile (`icc.ts` `buildIcc(ADOBE_RGB_PROFILE)`) inserted after its start marker, and the browser converts it like any tagged JPEG. Used for Import and Open folder thumbnails (`probe.worker.ts`) and for the decoder's preview (`extractPreview` in `decoder.worker.ts`) behind the Loupe, culling and Survey. | LightBench |
| JPEG files | A JPEG file with no ICC profile whose EXIF InteropIndex is `R03` is shown in the Library the way Develop already reads it (`chooseInputProfile`, darktable's colorin order), so its thumbnail and Loupe match Develop. | darktable `src/iop/colorin.c` (as `icc.ts`) |
| Test files | Of the test RAWs, the Sony ILCE-7M2 ARW is Adobe RGB (EXIF ColorSpace `0xFFFF`, InteropIndex `R03`; its 686,866-byte preview has no ICC or EXIF of its own). The ILCE-6300 ARW says sRGB (`R98`); both NEFs say sRGB in maker note `0x001e`. No test file has a Nikon Adobe RGB note: that case is covered by constructed TIFFs in the unit tests. | Checked with a small TIFF dump script |
| Existing thumbnails | Thumbnails already in the library were not redone by this part; since Part 6, a one-time background refresh redoes them (unedited photos only). | LightBench |

### GPS

| Behaviour | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Before | LightBench stored no GPS for any file, JPEG included (Export said “LightBench doesn't store it”). | LightBench 0.2 |
| RAWs | LibRaw's `imgother.parsed_gps` (exposed by the binding): degrees/minutes/seconds, reference letters as character codes. A position needs both letters: LibRaw sets `gpsparsed` as soon as the GPS IFD has any entry, and the D700 sample has a GPS IFD holding only its version. | LibRaw `src/metadata/exif_gps.cpp` `parse_gps_libraw` (lines 331–391, 0.22.1) |
| JPEGs | The GPS IFD (`0x8825`) of the JPEG's EXIF, tags 1–6. | EXIF; the checks follow LightCraft `crates/meta/src/exif.rs` `read_gps` (lines 125–139) |
| Stored | Catalog schema 12: `photos.gps_lat`, `gps_lon` (degrees, north and east positive) and `gps_alt` (metres), NULL when not recorded. Photos added before got none; since Part 6, a one-time background refresh reads it for them. | The existing migration pattern |
| Shown | A **GPS** line in the Library's info panel, e.g. “45.42153° N, 75.69723° W · 71 m”. There is no map panel. Exports still leave GPS out (the Export dialog says so). | LightBench |

### IPTC from JPEGs

| Behaviour | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Read | APP13 “Photoshop 3.0”, image resource `0x0404`: Object Name (2:05) → Title, Caption/Abstract (2:120) → Caption, By-line (2:80, repeated for several) → Creator, Copyright Notice (2:116), Headline (2:105), City (2:90), Province/State (2:95), Country (2:101), Keywords (2:25). IIM has no copyright URL. Text is UTF-8 when 1:90 says `ESC % G`, else UTF-8 when valid, else Latin-1. `web/src/engine/meta/iptc.ts`. | LightCraft `crates/meta/src/iptc.rs` `parse_iptc`, `container.rs` `photoshop_iptc` (line 130); the dataset → XMP mapping is Adobe's (XMP Toolkit SDK, `XMPFiles/source/FormatSupport/IPTC_Support.cpp`, `kKnownDataSets`) |
| Precedence | XMP (the sidecar, or the JPEG's own) wins; IPTC fills fields it leaves empty; then the camera's EXIF (Artist, Copyright, ImageDescription). IPTC keywords are used only when the XMP has none. | Adobe's XMP Toolkit, which Bridge and Photoshop use: `ReconcileIPTC.cpp` `Import2WayIPTC` (“Keep the existing XMP” when the IPTC digest matches or is missing) and `ReconcileTIFF.cpp` `Import3WayItems` (XMP, then IPTC, then EXIF for copyright, description and creator), which follow the Metadata Working Group guidelines. That Lightroom Classic reads JPEGs the same way is *unverified*: nothing reachable from here documents it. |
| Not done | When the IPTC was changed after the XMP was written (its MD5 digest, resource `0x0425`, doesn't match), Adobe's toolkit lets the IPTC win; LightBench doesn't check the digest and always lets XMP win. IPTC in RAWs is not read. | Follow-ups |

### Display P3 in Develop

| Behaviour | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| When | When `matchMedia('(color-gamut: p3)')` matches, the Develop canvas is configured as Display P3 (WebGPU `colorSpace: 'display-p3'`, or the read-back 2D canvas created in that colour space) and the display shader writes Display P3. On sRGB screens the canvas and the shader path are as before. | Media Queries 4 `color-gamut`; WebGPU `GPUCanvasConfiguration.colorSpace`. The idea is LightCraft's (`docs/display-profiles.md`, issue #787), done the browser's way, so none of its code is used |
| The maths | The Display P3 export's: linear Rec.2020 → linear Display P3 with `icc.ts` `rec2020ToProfile(DISPLAY_P3_PROFILE)`, clamped per channel, sRGB tone curve, as `export.wgsl` encodes a Display P3 export. `web/src/engine/gpu/display-space.ts`, `develop-display.wgsl`. | LightBench's export path |
| Histogram and clipping | Still judged in sRGB, whatever the screen. The clipping indicator colours are converted so they look the same. | Owner's requirement |
| Other canvases | The Library's Loupe, culling and Survey canvases use Display P3 on such screens (so tagged Adobe RGB previews keep their gamut), and so does the System Check's RAW test renderer. Thumbnails, exports and the white balance loupe stay sRGB. | LightBench |
| Changing screens | Moving the window to a screen with another gamut reconfigures the WebGPU canvas and redraws, as far as the browser re-evaluates the media query then (*unverified* with two real monitors). A read-back canvas keeps the colour space it was made with (the browser converts it) until Develop opens again. | LightBench |

### JPEG Tint +150

| Behaviour | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| The bug | On an sRGB JPEG at its as-shot 6500 K, Tint +150 asked for a white outside sRGB: the red multiplier came out −7.9 and the photo turned cyan. The same happens at other extremes, e.g. 1901 K at Tint 0 (blue multiplier about 4,800), and for RAWs (D700) at warm Temp with Tint far to the green side (a negative red multiplier). | [PHASE2.md](PHASE2.md); the whole Temp × Tint grid was measured here |
| darktable | No guard: `_xyz2mul` takes 1 / CAM as it is, and for non-raw input sRGB is the “camera” (`src/iop/temperature.c`, `_temp2mul` lines 452–469, `_prepare_matrices` lines 1411–1416). Only its spot picker clamps the coefficients to 0…8 (line 1946), and its coefficient parameters range 0…8 (`$MAX: 8.0`). | darktable |
| The fix | LightBench's own: the tint is limited to where every multiplier stays positive and within 8× of green either way for JPEGs (darktable's 0…8 range; the 1/8 floor is LightBench's, so both ends of the slider stop alike), and within 64× for RAWs (a guard only). The slider stops getting stronger at that edge instead of turning back; if no tint fits at that Temp, the white is moved in camera RGB towards neutral up to the limit. Settings within the limit are computed exactly as before. `engine/color/temperature.ts`. | LightBench |
| What changes | JPEGs: only settings that gave a multiplier above 8× or below 1/8 of green (at 6500 K: Tint above about +95, where red was between 8 and 9.5 and climbing towards the breakdown, and below about −135; also very warm or very cool Temp). RAWs (D700, whole grid): only Temp ≤ 2850 K with Tint −40 or further left, which gave negative or runaway multipliers. No new process version: `process.ts` lets fixes for broken output skip one. JPEG settings between about +95 and the old breakdown were not broken and changed slightly (red 9.5 → 8 at +100); the owner chose to apply the limit to all edits rather than keep old edits' exact look behind a new process version (2026-10-12). | LightBench |
| Direction | Tint + is magenta, − is green, as the slider's track and help say (“Right: More magenta (pink).”). | `develop/help.ts`, `BasicPanel.tsx` |

**Verified** (headless Chromium 141 with software WebGPU (SwiftShader), on this branch merged with main at `f01275b` (Lens and Upright); RAWs from immich-app/test-assets; files made for this part and not committed: `IMG_1.NEF` (= D700 `philadelphia.nef`) with `IMG_1.JPG` (= `test-srgb.jpg` plus an EXIF GPS IFD at 45°25′17.51″ N 75°41′50.03″ W, 70.5 m, and IPTC title, caption, creator, copyright, headline, city, state, country and two keywords), `BOTH.JPG` (the same IPTC plus embedded XMP with a title and a keyword), `GPS.NEF` (`philadelphia.nef` with its GPS IFD replaced by one at 33°51′35.64″ S 151°12′40.32″ E, 58 m), `ADOBE.ARW` (ILCE-7M2) and `SRGB.ARW` (ILCE-6300); scratch scripts `e2e-extras` (23 of 23 checks passed), `e2e-tint` and `e2e-p3`):
- **Twins:** Open folder stacked the pair and wrote `IMG_1.xmp` and `IMG_1.JPG.xmp`, both naming the stack with the RAW on top. 2 stars on the NEF and 5 on the JPEG landed in their own files and stayed. The JPEG's IPTC keywords were in its own file only. Changing `IMG_1.JPG.xmp` to 1 star outside LightBench gave “1 photo was changed by another app”; **Show photos** showed only IMG_1.JPG; **Read changes** gave it 1 star and left the NEF at 2. Changing `IMG_1.xmp` was reported for the NEF only.
- **GPS:** GPS.NEF showed “33.85990° S, 151.21120° E · 58 m”, IMG_1.JPG “45.42153° N, 75.69723° W · 71 m”; there was no GPS line for the A6300 ARW or the D700 NEF (version-only GPS IFD).
- **IPTC:** IMG_1.JPG's Metadata panel held all eight fields from IPTC and the keywords iptc-one and iptc-two. BOTH.JPG: title “XMP title” and only the keyword xmp-kw (from XMP), city and caption from IPTC.
- **Preview colour:** ADOBE.ARW's thumbnail matched its preview read as Adobe RGB (mean difference 3.42 levels, against 4.25 when read as sRGB; mean saturation 12.8, against 13.1 as Adobe RGB and 10.2 as sRGB, the old look). SRGB.ARW's matched its preview as sRGB (2.24 levels, against 9.59 as Adobe RGB), as before. The decoder's preview of ADOBE.ARW carried “Adobe RGB (1998)”; SRGB.ARW's and the NEF's had no profile added. (This test photo is not very colourful, so the differences are small.)
- **Tint** (`test-srgb.jpg`, mean colour of the photo in Develop): 0 → (102.1, 111.6, 118.1), +50 → (130.5, 111.9, 131.0), +100, +120 and +150 → (172.4, 115.9, 141.4), magenta. On main: +100 (176.3, 117.3, 142.2), +120 (190.4, 183.8, 169.8), +150 (6.5, 65.9, 83.4), inverted.
- **Display P3** (`e2e-p3`: the Develop canvas read back in its own colour space; `--force-color-profile=display-p3-d65` made `matchMedia('(color-gamut: p3)')` true, by default it was false): on the sRGB screen, the canvas (890 × 818) and the histogram were byte-identical to main's, for `test-srgb.jpg` (SHA-256 e2f1d202…) and for the D700 NEF with main's default lens correction (e5ec61f8…). On the P3 screen the canvas was Display P3: every pixel inside sRGB was within 1 level of the sRGB render converted to Display P3 (728,020 pixels, 66 % identical), neutral greys were identical and the histogram was identical to the sRGB one; main kept drawing sRGB there. With Saturation +100, 23,587 of the 27,089 pixels that the sRGB render clips held colour beyond it on the P3 screen.
- **Unit tests** (`npx vitest run`: 455 passing, 43 new): `naming.test.ts`, `find-sidecar.test.ts` (twin names, reading order, borrowing; XMP over IPTC over EXIF), `catalog.worker.test.ts` (owners in `sidecarStates` and `sidecarSource`; the schema 12 upgrade; GPS stored), `preview-space.test.ts` (constructed TIFFs: Nikon type 3 and type 1 notes, R03, DNG, LibRaw-style EXIF; an ICC profile wins; the inserted profile parses as Adobe RGB), `gps.test.ts`, `iptc.test.ts` (LightCraft's cases plus the location fields and a whole APP13), `temperature.test.ts` (13 temperatures × 31 tints for the D700, sRGB and Adobe RGB JPEGs: positive, finite, within the limit, unchanged inside it, Tint never reversing; the PHASE2 case), `settings.test.ts` (Tint direction now over the full ±150 for the JPEG) and `display-space.test.ts` (the media query and its change listener, the export's matrix, indicator colours, the shader's uniform layout). `npx tsc -b`, `npx oxlint` (only the two existing warnings) and `npm run build` passed.

**Limitations:**
- The software WebGPU used for testing sometimes showed a JPEG black on the first Develop open, on main as well; the scripts open it again then.
- A RAW removed from the library but left on disk no longer counts as a twin when LightBench writes (the catalog decides), so its JPEG would write `NAME.xmp`. When a folder is opened, the files on disk decide which sidecar is read.
- Moving the window between screens, the Display P3 Loupe and culling canvases, and the WebGPU (not read-back) canvas path were not exercised end to end (the test browser always used read-back); only unit tests cover the screen-change listener.
- For the D700 at 1901 K, darktable's formula itself turns back on the green side before it breaks down (Tint 0 → −50 makes the result less green); the limit can't undo that.
- No test file has a Nikon Adobe RGB maker note, GPS in a RAW or IPTC in a JPEG: those cases were constructed.

## Part 5: Phone DNGs (linear DNG) and Fujifilm X-Trans

**What it does.** The two RAW layouts LightBench used to refuse now open, develop, thumbnail and export like any other RAW:
- **Linear DNGs** (Apple ProRAW, many phone DNGs, the linear output of DNG converters): three colours per pixel, already demosaiced, but still scene-linear camera RGB. They go through the RAW steps (black and white levels, white balance, highlight reconstruction, the camera matrix) without a demosaic, not through the JPEG path. Their colour comes from the file's own DNG colour model: the two calibration matrices interpolated for the photo's white balance.
- **Fujifilm X-Trans RAFs**: darktable's default X-Trans demosaic, **Markesteijn 1-pass**, translated to WebGPU, with the raw steps and the clipping indicators taught the 6×6 pattern.
- **Bayer DNGs that LibRaw has no colour matrix for** (e.g. Google Pixel phone DNGs) used to fail with “Matrix is not invertible”; they now use the DNG colour model too.

Old edits of Bayer photos render byte-identically (below). ProRAW's embedded semantic masks (sky, person, skin, hair mattes) are a follow-up for the Phase 4 masks, not built here.

### Decoding

| Behaviour | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Telling layouts apart | LibRaw's `filters`: 0 = no colour filter array, 9 = X-Trans, larger = a 2×2 Bayer pattern. `SensorData.layout` is `'bayer'`, `'xtrans'` or `'linear'` (`engine/raw/types.ts`). Foveon and monochrome files are still refused with a reason; so are linear files that aren't DNGs. | LibRaw; darktable `src/iop/demosaic.c` (`filters == 9u`) |
| Linear DNG data | LibRaw unpacks a LinearRaw DNG as 3 or 4 values per pixel (`getColor3Image` / `getColor4Image`; every test file came as 4). LightBench keeps R, G, B per pixel (uint16) for the visible area, black per colour, and the DNG WhiteLevel as white. | darktable reads them the same way: rawspeed's 3-channel image (`src/imageio/imageio_rawspeed.cc` `dt_imageio_open_rawspeed_sraw`, line 495) |
| X-Trans pattern | The 6×6 colours from LibRaw's `COLOR()` for the visible area (where the data starts), checked to be an X-Trans pattern. Black is per 2×2 position as for Bayer: darktable's rawprepare does the same for X-Trans (`src/iop/rawprepare.c` `_BL`, line 314). White from darktable's camera database (all four test bodies are in it). | darktable |
| DNG colour tags | Read from the file's first IFD by a small TIFF reader (`engine/raw/dng-tags.ts`): ColorMatrix1/2, CameraCalibration1/2, ForwardMatrix1/2, AnalogBalance, AsShotNeutral, AsShotWhiteXY, CalibrationIlluminant1/2, BaselineExposure. The WebAssembly binding doesn't expose LibRaw's own `dng_color`, and LibRaw leaves its `cam_xyz` at zero for DNGs of cameras it doesn't know (the Pixel and iPhone files). | DNG Specification 1.7, chapter 6 |
| Which DNGs use the DNG model | Linear DNGs, and Bayer DNGs whose LibRaw matrix is missing. Bayer DNGs that already opened (e.g. the Ricoh GR III) keep LibRaw's matrix, so their saved edits don't change. | LightBench's choice (process versions, Part 1) |

### The DNG colour model

| Behaviour | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| The model | XYZtoCamera = AnalogBalance · CameraCalibration · ColorMatrix, each interpolated between the two calibration illuminants by the inverse colour temperature of the white; camera → XYZ (D50) through ForwardMatrix when the file has one, else the inverse of XYZtoCamera and a Bradford adaptation; Bradford D50 → D65 into LightBench's linear Rec.2020; scaled so a white-balanced neutral (1, 1, 1) stays (1, 1, 1), as LibRaw's `rgb_cam` does. As-shot white from AsShotWhiteXY, else from AsShotNeutral by the specification's iteration. `engine/color/dng.ts`. | Ported from LightCraft `crates/raw/src/color.rs`, `crates/color/src/cct.rs` and `lib.rs` (MIT, see THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.md). darktable uses one matrix, the one closest to D65, without interpolating (`src/common/exif.cc`, lines 2647-2709, “FIXME interpolate the matrixes”) |
| White balance | The camera matrix follows the white balance: `stageOptions` hands the engine the matrix for the chosen multipliers, so the raw stage runs again when Temp or Tint change (as for any RAW). Temperature/tint ⇄ multipliers keep darktable's maths (`temperature.ts`, with Part 4's limit), with XYZ → camera taken at that white (`multipliersOf` / `temperatureOf` in `develop/source.ts`); the white balance picker uses the same. | darktable `src/iop/temperature.c`; LightCraft for the white-dependent matrix |
| Not applied | BaselineExposure (read; darktable doesn't apply it either), the DNG profile look (ProfileHueSatMap, ProfileLookTable, ProfileToneCurve), ProRAW's ProfileGainTableMap (local tone mapping), a third illuminant (DNG 1.6), DNG opcodes beyond what Part 2's lens corrections read. | Follow-ups |

### The raw stage on the GPU

| Behaviour | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Linear DNGs | `raw-linear.wgsl`: black/white per colour and white balance, then highlight reconstruction: “inpaint opposed” uses darktable's linear variant (the opposed colours of the same pixel, chroma measured near clipped areas from more than 30 samples), “clip highlights” clips all colours at the lowest level; then camera RGB → Rec.2020. No demosaic (Develop's Advanced section says so instead of showing the demosaic menu). | darktable `src/iop/hlreconstruct/opposed.c` `_process_linear_opposed` (lines 83-233) and `_calc_linear_refavg` (lines 49-55); `src/iop/rawprepare.c` (line 383); demosaic off for these files (`src/iop/demosaic.c`, line 1602). One difference: darktable's clipped-colour mask tests the first channel for all three colours (line 145), which looks unintended; LightBench tests each colour |
| X-Trans raw prepare and highlights | The Bayer kernels of `raw-pipeline.wgsl`, compiled a second time with a WGSL override (`XTRANS`) that takes colours from the 6×6 pattern; Bayer photos run the shader as before. | darktable `data/kernels/basic.cl` (`highlights_initmask` line 358, `highlights_chroma` 436, `highlights_opposed` 478 take the X-Trans colour) |
| Markesteijn 1-pass | `demosaic-xtrans.wgsl`: darktable's OpenCL kernels translated (initial copy, green min/max, green in four directions, red/blue at the solitary greens in six passes, red for blue and vice versa, the 2×2 green blocks, YPbPr, derivatives, homogeneity maps and their 5×5 sums, the average of the most homogeneous directions). One pass is darktable's default for X-Trans; Markesteijn 3-pass was added in Part 9. The 12-pixel border and the fast method use darktable's linear interpolation (`vng_lin_interpolate`). | darktable `data/kernels/demosaic_markesteijn.cl` (Ulrich Pegelow, 2016), `src/iop/demosaicing/xtrans.c` `process_markesteijn_cl` (lines 1632-2047; hexagon map 1674-1700), `data/kernels/demosaic_vng.cl` (lines 21-136); default from `src/iop/demosaic.c` lines 1591-1592; adapted from dcraw 9.20 |
| Strips | The photo is demosaiced in horizontal strips (about 190 MB of working buffers each, 120 bytes per pixel), each reading 18 extra rows on both sides, a multiple of 6 so the pattern's phase holds. A demosaiced pixel depends on input at most 15 rows away, so each strip's output equals a whole-photo run (checked below: identical scene with 36-row, 600-row and default strips). | LightBench (darktable's CPU path tiles the same way; its OpenCL path doesn't) |
| Other differences | The kernels read global memory directly instead of workgroup tiles (reads outside the strip are 0, as darktable's tiles are 0 outside the photo); darktable's threshold, homogeneity-maximum and accumulation kernels are merged into two loops over the four directions. | LightBench |
| Demosaic menu | The stored value stays `rcd` / `bilinear`; on an X-Trans photo they mean Markesteijn 1-pass / linear interpolation, as darktable maps a Bayer method to its X-Trans counterpart (`src/iop/demosaic.c`, lines 1505-1506). The menu shows those names. (Part 9 adds `markesteijn3`, Markesteijn 3-pass, on X-Trans photos.) | darktable |

### Everything else

| Behaviour | Detail |
|---|---|
| Sensor clipping (Shift+O) and “lost detail” (J) | The overlay reads photosite colours from the 6×6 pattern; “lost detail” tests the 3×3 block (which holds all three colours) on X-Trans, and a linear DNG's own three values per pixel. On a linear DNG, “mark with sensor colour” marks the clipped colours. |
| Thumbnails | From the embedded preview first, as for every RAW; files without one show the placeholder until edited. |
| Lens corrections (Part 2) | Work on both layouts, after the demosaic. The Fujifilm test files were corrected with the camera's own data (TCA and vignetting; “Corrected with the camera's own data (Fujifilm …)”). The phone and converted DNGs found no profile (Lensfun has no Pixel; the converted Ricoh file carries no focal length) and stay uncorrected, with the reason shown. |
| Process version | Still 1: these files couldn't be developed before, so no saved edit changes (noted in `engine/develop/process.ts`). |
| System Check | Reports the layout; its Phase 0 GPU speed test is Bayer-only and is skipped for the other layouts. |

**Test files** (for testing only, not in the repository): X-Trans: Fujifilm X100F `20241017_0191.RAF`, X-T2 `DSCF3323.RAF`, X-T4 `DSCF9838.RAF` (the `dtai` rawdenoise samples) and X100V `X100V_compressed.RAF` from immich test-assets (portrait orientation; a black frame). Bayer DNG: Google Pixel (“sailfish”) `payload_N000.dng` from Google's HDR+ burst dataset (10-bit Bayer, no preview) and Ricoh GR III `Ricoh_GR3-450.DNG` (immich test-assets). Linear DNG: **no real Apple ProRAW or other linear phone DNG could be obtained** (raw.pixls.us is blocked from the test machine). Two linear DNGs were made from real Bayer files with LibRaw's AHD demosaic in camera RGB and the source's colour tags (`scratchpad/rawformats/mkdng.py`): “ProRAW-style” from the Pixel file (IFD0 an 8-bit JPEG preview rendered by LibRaw, SubIFD the 16-bit LinearRaw image, as ProRAW is laid out) and “converter-style” from the Ricoh file (IFD0 the LinearRaw image, no preview, ForwardMatrix1/2 derived from its ColorMatrices). An iPhone 12 Pro ProRAW header (`IMG_1361.dng` from exiv2's test data, 64 KB: a real ProRAW's tags, the image data cut off) was used for the tags only. Bayer regression: Nikon D700 and D80 NEF, Sony A6300 and A7 II ARW (immich test-assets) and the Ricoh DNG.

**Verified** (headless Chromium 141, software WebGPU (SwiftShader); scratch scripts in `scratchpad/rawformats/`):
- **Old edits unchanged** (`e2e-oldedit`): on the code without this part (origin/main at `c147f74`), A.NEF (D700) was edited in Develop (Exposure +0.5, Contrast +20, Shadows +10, Saturation −10, straightened 1.35°), its sidecar kept, and every Bayer file rendered with the defaults and with A's edit; then again with this part merged. Full-size exports had identical SHA-256 (A edited `3a8d47ab…`; B, C, D and the Ricoh DNG with the defaults; the Ricoh DNG with A's edit), every 1024 px export and 512 px thumbnail differed in 0 bytes, the histogram statistics were identical, and so was A's Develop canvas (890 × 662, 0 bytes differ). The same check passed against the two earlier states of main this branch was merged with (`ad22308` and `f01275b`). On main, the Pixel DNG failed with “Matrix is not invertible” and the X-Trans file with “not supported yet”; both develop now.
- **Markesteijn against LibRaw's own** (`xtcheck.mjs`, `xtcompare.py`; X100F, white balance 1, no clipping, camera RGB): in six 256 × 256 crops, LightBench's Markesteijn differs from LibRaw's `xtrans_interpolate` (dcraw's Markesteijn, 1 pass) by a mean absolute 0.80–1.43 % of the mean level in the four normally exposed crops and 3.3–6.9 % in two very dark ones. For scale, LibRaw's 3-pass differs from its 1-pass by 0.49–0.89 % (1.9–2.9 % dark), and linear interpolation differs from LibRaw's 1-pass by about 6 %. darktable's version differs from dcraw's on purpose (YPbPr instead of CIELab in the homogeneity test, floats), so identity isn't expected. LightBench's linear interpolation matches LibRaw's `lin_interpolate` to 0.11–0.15 % (0.3–0.8 % dark). Side-by-side crops at 2× looked alike and sharper than linear interpolation, without maze or zipper artefacts (checked by eye).
- **Strips:** the X100F scene texture hashed identically with the default strips, 36-row strips and 600-row strips.
- **Through the app** (`e2e-formats.mjs`: Open folder, Develop, Advanced section, Shift+O, Exposure +0.5, Export PNG at 100 %, back to the Library; final run on the merged code): 24 of 24 checks passed for the X100F, X-T4 and X100V RAFs, the ProRAW-style and converter-style linear DNGs and the Pixel Bayer DNG. Each opened in Develop (the RAFs in 75–79 s, the DNGs in 33–38 s on SwiftShader), showed the right demosaic menu (“Markesteijn 1-pass”, the linear-DNG note, “RCD”), drew the sensor-clipping overlay, recorded the edit in its sidecar (exposure 0.5, process 1) with a new 512 px thumbnail, and exported at full size (6032 × 4028, 6246 × 4170, the X100V turned to 4170 × 6246 by its orientation, 4048 × 3044, 6020 × 4024, 4048 × 3044). Library thumbnails came from the embedded previews (512 × 341, the X100V 341 × 512, the ProRAW-style 512 × 385); the two files without a preview showed the placeholder until edited, then 512 px renders. Screenshots of Develop, the sensor-clipping overlay and the exports were checked by eye.
- **Against the camera's JPEG** (colour and brightness sanity check, at 192 px): X100F night scene under sodium lamps: our export 0.80 EV darker than Fujifilm's JPEG, 12 × 8 block correlation of log luminance 0.995, mean colour shares R/G/B 0.544/0.384/0.072 against 0.599/0.343/0.058 (LightBench's as-shot rendering a little less warm); X-T4 dusk scene: 0.43 EV brighter, correlation 0.996, shares 0.100/0.246/0.653 against 0.056/0.217/0.727 (LightBench less blue than the camera). The ProRAW-style file against its preview (made by LibRaw, not by a phone, so only a consistency check): 0.68 EV brighter, correlation 0.987. Renders were checked by eye against the previews.
- **The DNG model is consistent:** the linear DNG made from the Pixel file and the Pixel Bayer DNG itself rendered the same picture (mean luma 111.75 and 111.77 of 255, colourfulness 0.167 and 0.169), as-shot white 5092 K / tint 1.026 for both.
- **Real ProRAW tags** (the iPhone 12 Pro header): read as a linear DNG with ColorMatrix1 (standard light A) and ColorMatrix2 (D65); as-shot white 5221 K / tint 1.007. Its image data isn't in the file, so it rendered black.
- **Speed** (SwiftShader is a CPU emulation of a GPU, so these numbers say nothing about a real GPU): the X100F's raw stage took 43 s with Markesteijn (white balance 1, clip) and 52–65 s with the default settings (opposed highlights), 4.7 s with linear interpolation only, and 100 s with 36-row strips. The 12 MP Pixel DNG's raw stage took 6–8 s (Bayer RCD), its linear twin 2 s. Not measured on a real GPU.
- **Unit tests** (`npx vitest run`, 491 passing): `xtrans.test.ts` (21: pattern checks, colour lookup with negative coordinates, packing, the solitary green and the hexagon map for four phases of the pattern (each red/blue photosite's six offsets land on greens, each 2×2-block green's on red/blue), strip coverage and overlap), `dng.test.ts` (11, mostly LightCraft's: neutral to white and primaries recovered, dual-illuminant weights in either order, white ⇄ neutral round trip, ForwardMatrix path equals the ColorMatrix path, CameraCalibration and AnalogBalance, temperature/tint round trip through the white-dependent matrix), `dng-tags.test.ts` (4: both byte orders, non-DNG files, 4-colour and singular matrices, AsShotWhiteXY), and the display uniform layout test extended. `npx tsc -b`, `npx oxlint` (the two existing warnings) and `npm run build` passed.

**Limitations and unverified:**
- **No real linear phone DNG was tested**: Apple ProRAW and Android linear DNGs are untested beyond the ProRAW header's tags. Real ProRAW stores 12-bit lossless JPEG tiles with a LinearizationTable; LibRaw read that header's structure (as 4 colours per pixel) but its pixels weren't there to check.
- ProRAW renders without its profile look (ProfileToneCurve, HueSatMap/LookTable), its local tone map (ProfileGainTableMap) and BaselineExposure (−0.30 EV on the iPhone header), as darktable does. Lightroom applies at least BaselineExposure and the profile (from general knowledge, not checked), so ProRAW will look flatter than, and differ in brightness from, Apple's rendering.
- Bayer DNGs that already opened keep LibRaw's single matrix; giving them the DNG model needs a new process version.
- Only two illuminants are read (DNG 1.6's third and IlluminantData are not).
- darktable's other X-Trans methods (VNG, frequency domain chroma, dual demosaic) aren't offered (Markesteijn 3-pass is, since Part 9). The X-Trans raw stage is heavy (four RGB directions plus maps, about 190 MB per strip); on SwiftShader opening a 24 MP RAF takes over a minute. Real-GPU timing is unknown.
- The sensor-clipping overlay on linear DNGs drew without errors, but wasn't checked against a known clipped area; on X-Trans it marked the X100F's street lamps (checked by eye).
- The X100V test file is a black frame, so it only checked orientation and the pipeline.
- Fujifilm's own lens corrections were applied on real RAFs (Part 2 had checked them only with made-up data), but their accuracy wasn't measured.
- ProRAW's semantic masks are left for Phase 4.

## Part 6: Rating keys, and a one-time refresh of older photos

**What it does.** Two follow-ups chosen by the owner (2026-10-12):
- **] and [** give the photo one star more or less, and **Shift+0–5** sets the rating and goes to the next photo, as in Lightroom Classic. They work wherever ratings can be set: the Library grid and Loupe, culling, Compare and Survey. (Develop has no rating controls, so nothing changes there.)
- **Photos already in the library get the extras bundle's improvements, once, in the background**: their GPS position is read (Part 4 only read it for photos added afterwards), and a thumbnail made from an Adobe RGB camera preview is made again with the right colours. Edited photos keep their thumbnails, and nothing else about any photo changes.

### Rating keys

| Behaviour | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| The keys | In both shortcut sets: **]** One star more, **[** One star less, **Shift+0** … **Shift+5** “No stars / n stars, then the next photo”. Each is an ordinary action in `settings/actions.ts`, so it can be changed in Settings › Keyboard, and it shows in the **?** list, in command search (“star”, “next photo”, “shift+3”) and in the Library and culling hint lines (“[ ] ± star · Shift+0–5 stars and next”). | Lightroom Classic: “Set star rating and go to next photo: Shift+1–5”, and ] / [ to increase / decrease the rating: Adobe's “Keyboard shortcuts for Lightroom Classic” seen through web-search excerpts (helpx.adobe.com didn't resolve from the build environment, as in Phase 2): **Adobe (excerpt)**. Lightroom's Shift+0 was **not checked**; it is LightBench's, for symmetry with 0. |
| Several photos | In the Library, ] and [ change each selected photo from its own rating (1 and 3 stars become 2 and 4); 0 and 5 stay put. Shift+0–5 rates the selection and moves to the next photo. | Lightroom's behaviour with several photos was not checked |
| Moving on, never twice | Shift+0–5 moves exactly once, whatever else is on: with Caps Lock in the Library (Caps Lock already advances after a flag, star or label key, Phase 2 Part 2) and with culling's Auto-advance on or off. In Compare it moves the Candidate on when the Candidate is active and leaves the Select where it is, as Auto-advance does; in Survey (where Auto-advance is off) it moves to the next tile. ] and [ behave like the other rating keys: they advance only with Caps Lock or Auto-advance. | LightBench |
| Ctrl+[ and Ctrl+] | Still rotate in Develop (Part 1); the rating keys are plain [ and ]. | — |
| Room for a brush | In Lightroom, [ and ] change the brush size while a brush tool is active (an Adobe employee's answer in the Adobe Community, 2017, for the Spot Removal and Adjustment brushes, seen through search: **secondary**). The rating actions run only in the Library and culling contexts; a brush will be one more Develop context (like `crop` and `guided`), where they don't run, so it can take [ and ] without a conflict. A unit test checks that plain [ and ] are unused in Develop, cropping and Guided Upright. | LightBench |
| Shift with a number | Shortcuts follow the printed character (Phase 2, Part 12), and Shift is part of the character for digits and symbols, so Shift+3 on a US keyboard arrives as “#”. A new rule: Shift on a digit key that then types a symbol (“#” US, “§” German, “№” Russian) is also read as **Shift+3** by the key's position (`code` Digit3), after the symbol itself, so a key bound to “#” would still win. Where the digit itself needs Shift (French AZERTY), Shift+3 types “3” and stays the plain 3 stars, as before. Recording a key in Settings stores “Shift+3”, not “#”. The numeric keypad is unchanged. `settings/chords.ts`. | LightBench |
| [ and ] on other layouts | [ and ] are the characters, wherever they are: AltGr+8 / AltGr+9 on a German keyboard, AltGr+5 / AltGr+) on a French one. On layouts without Latin letters the key at the US [ position works (Russian х). | Phase 2 rule |
| Fix: accented letters | The US-position fallback was meant for layouts that type no Latin letters, but it also caught accented letters: on a German keyboard **Ü** would have lowered the rating (it sits where US keyboards have [), and Ctrl+Ä made a virtual copy (Ä sits at the US ' position); on a French keyboard the unshifted é, è, ç and à keys rated 2 stars, gave the yellow or blue label or cleared the rating, while &, ", ' and ( did nothing. Accented Latin letters now count as letters (`\p{Script=Latin}`), so they follow the printed letter like any other. | LightBench |

### The one-time refresh

| Behaviour | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Which photos | Catalog schema 13 adds `photos.extras_read`: the upgrade gives every photo already there 0, photos added from then on get 1 (the pattern of `keywords_read` and `metadata_read`). A photo is set to 1 once read, so the refresh runs once per library and picks up where it stopped. A library restored from a backup made before the refresh has 0s again (its catalog is upgraded on opening), so the refresh runs again, also replacing the backup's old thumbnails. Virtual copies take their original's GPS and refresh state (before, a copy had no GPS at all). | LightBench |
| What is read | Each file once (original and virtual copies together), with the probe workers Open folder and Import use (`import/probe.worker.ts`): GPS from LibRaw's `parsed_gps` for RAWs and from the EXIF GPS IFD for JPEGs, exactly as when a photo is added. The probe now also says whether its thumbnail was converted from Adobe RGB (`thumbAdobeRgb`). | Part 4 |
| GPS | Stored when the file has a position; a position already stored is never cleared. | — |
| Thumbnails | Replaced only when the new thumbnail came from JPEG data that `preview-space.ts` reads as Adobe RGB (a RAW's camera preview; or a JPEG file with InteropIndex R03 and no ICC profile, which Part 4 also shows as Adobe RGB) **and** the photo has no edit. An edited photo's thumbnail is a render of its edit (Develop and Paste/Sync render it), told apart by its row in the `edits` table, checked just before writing. Other thumbnails, sRGB ones included, are left as they are. | LightBench |
| Never changed | Ratings, flags, labels, keywords, metadata, edits, collections, stacks and sidecars: the refresh writes only `gps_lat`, `gps_lon`, `gps_alt`, `extras_read` and thumbnail files, and schedules no sidecar write (GPS is not in sidecars). | — |
| Folders without permission | Checked with `queryPermission` only; the browser allows asking only after a click, so nothing is asked and nothing is shown. Their photos wait and are done the next time the folder can be read: after Reconnect or Open folder in the same visit, or on a later visit where the browser still allows it. A file that can't be found now also waits; a file the probe can't read is marked done (adding it again would fail the same way). | Sidecar sync's `needsPermission` holds writes per folder the same way, but asks; here it doesn't, as the owner asked |
| When | When the library opens, when Open folder finishes and when a folder is reconnected. It waits while Open folder is reading. | LightBench |
| Not slowing the UI | Two probe workers (Open folder uses up to six), two files at a time, a yield between them; the page only writes the results. | LightBench |
| The note | A small, quiet box in the Library's left panel, styled like Open folder's progress: “Updating older photos (location, thumbnail colours): 3 of 7” with a bar and **Pause**. Pause stops after the files being read (“Updating older photos is paused: 4 left.” with **Resume**); the rest is done with Resume or the next time LightBench opens. When it has finished, and only if something changed: “Older photos updated: location for 2 photos, 2 thumbnails with corrected colours.” with OK. Nothing is shown when there is nothing to do, or when only folders without permission are left. `library/refresh.ts`, `library/RefreshProgress.tsx`. | Open folder's `IndexProgress` and Paste's `BatchProgress` (Stop, then a summary until OK) |

**Verified** (headless Chromium 141, software WebGPU (SwiftShader); scratch scripts in `scratchpad/followups-a/`; all re-run after merging main at `532cd0b`):
- **Refresh** (`build-old.mjs`, `refresh.mjs`, 43 of 43 checks). A library was built with main's code from before the extras bundle (`f01275b`, catalog schema 11) in a persistent browser profile. Folder **Photos**: ADOBE.ARW (Sony ILCE-7M2, Adobe RGB preview) rated 2; EDITED.ARW (the same file) rated 3 and edited in Develop (Exposure +0.5, so its thumbnail is a render); SRGB.ARW (ILCE-6300); GPS.NEF (D700 with a constructed GPS IFD); NOGPS.NEF (D700, GPS IFD with only its version); GPSJ.JPG (constructed EXIF GPS); R03.JPG (`test-srgb.jpg` without its ICC profile, with EXIF ColorSpace 0xFFFF and InteropIndex R03). Folder **Other**: GPS2.NEF. Both rated photos had sidecars. The same profile was then opened with this branch on the same origin, with **Other**'s read permission refused (`queryPermission` patched to “prompt”):
  - the note appeared (“… 0 of 7”); **Pause** left 4 files, and nothing more was read in 4 s; **Resume** finished: “Older photos updated: location for 2 photos, 2 thumbnails with corrected colours.”, 6–7 s after the page loaded; the page had one long task (55 ms) during all of it;
  - GPS.NEF −33.85990, 151.21120, 58 m (the info panel showed “33.85990° S, 151.21120° E · 58 m”); GPSJ.JPG 45.42153, −75.69723, 70.5 m; NOGPS.NEF none; GPS2.NEF none and still waiting;
  - new thumbnails for ADOBE.ARW and R03.JPG only; the thumbnails of EDITED.ARW (edited, same Adobe RGB file), SRGB.ARW, both NEFs, GPSJ.JPG and GPS2.NEF were identical (SHA-256); ADOBE.ARW's mean saturation (max − min per pixel) went from 10.17 to 12.79;
  - every catalog row was identical apart from the GPS columns (ratings 2 and 3 kept), edits and keywords identical, both sidecars identical in bytes and file date;
  - with permission back, a reload finished Other (“location for 1 photo”), GPS2.NEF's thumbnail unchanged; a further reload showed no note and changed nothing (catalog, thumbnails and sidecars compared);
  - the same files opened afresh as a new folder got thumbnails byte-identical to the refreshed ones, and weren't refreshed again.
- **Restore** (`backup-old.mjs`, `restore-new.mjs`, 20 of 20): a backup made with the pre-extras code, restored over the refreshed library with this branch. After the reload the refresh ran again (“location for 3 photos, 2 thumbnails …”); ratings and edits were the backup's; every thumbnail and position equalled the first refresh's; sidecars unchanged.
- **Keys** (`keys.mjs`, 39 of 39): in the grid, ] ] → 2 and [ → 1 with the focus kept; ] on two selected photos 1 → 2 and 0 → 1; [ stops at 0, ] at 5; Shift+3 (“#”) rated 3 and focused the next photo, Shift+0 cleared and moved; Caps Lock + Shift+4 moved once (a synthetic event with `modifierCapsLock`); Caps Lock + 2 and Caps Lock + ] moved once; plain 3 didn't move. Russian х at the [ position lowered the rating; German Ü and + did nothing; French Shift+3 (“3”) rated without moving and the unshifted 3 key (") did nothing; German Shift+3 (“§”) rated. Loupe: [ then Shift+1. The ? list showed “One star more ]”, “One star less [” and “3 stars, then the next photo Shift+3”, found the six Shift actions by “next photo” and only that one by “shift+3”; command search “one star more” + Enter raised 2 → 3. Culling with Auto-advance on: Shift+2, Caps Lock + Shift+3 and ] each moved exactly one; with it off, ] stayed, Shift+4 moved exactly one, 5 stayed. Compare: Shift+1 on the Candidate moved it from 6 to 7 of 8, [ on the Select left both in place. Survey: Shift+2 moved from NOGPS.NEF to R03.JPG, ] stayed. Develop: ], [ and Shift+5 changed no rating, and Ctrl+] still rotated. The Lightroom set: ] ] Shift+5. A custom key K for “One star more” worked, and ] then didn't.
- **Phase 2's keyboard script** (Part 12, its OK click scoped to the index progress): 86 of 89. The 3 that fail (Develop canvas backing size at 125 %, and at 125 % and 90 % with DPR 2) fail identically on unmodified main (`d90a744`), so they predate this part (not investigated here; Part 9: the test checked before the lens-corrected first frame was drawn, the canvas is fine).
- Screenshots of the note (running, paused, done), the ? list, command search, the hint lines and culling were checked by eye.
- **Unit tests** (`npx vitest run`, 500 passing, 9 new): `keymap.test.ts` (Shift+digit chords on US, German, Russian and French layouts, recording, keypad; [ and ] on German, Russian and with Ctrl; accented letters don't fall back; the new keys in all culling contexts and both sets; plain [ and ] unused in Develop, cropping and Guided Upright), `store.test.ts` (the “Shift+0–5” hint), `catalog.worker.test.ts` (schema 13 marks existing photos only and changes nothing else; pending photos by file with copies; GPS stored without touching ratings, flags, labels, keywords, edits or sidecar records; a stored position never cleared; virtual copies take GPS and state), `refresh.test.ts` (grouping by file, GPS from the probe, the thumbnail rule). `npx tsc -b`, `npx oxlint` (the two existing warnings) and `npm run build` passed.

**Limitations and unverified:**
- Lightroom's keys were checked through search excerpts of Adobe's page, not the page itself or Lightroom; Shift+0 and Lightroom's behaviour with several photos selected were not checked.
- On layouts where digits need Shift (French AZERTY) the default Shift+0–5 can't be typed: Shift+3 is the plain 3. Such users can give the actions other keys in Settings. Real non-US keyboards weren't used (synthetic key events, as in Part 12). On a Mac keyboard where [ needs Option, it arrives as Alt+[ (as \ already did); not verified on a Mac.
- An edited photo whose thumbnail is still its camera preview (an edit read from a sidecar when the photo was added, or Paste while its folder was disconnected) keeps the old thumbnail, because edited photos are never touched. It is redone the next time it is edited.
- The edit check and the thumbnail write aren't atomic: an edit saved in the moment between them would show the preview until its next save (not seen; not tested).
- Folders without permission were simulated by patching `queryPermission` (the test folders live in the browser's private storage, which always has permission); a real folder whose permission lapsed wasn't tried.
- A missing file stays pending and is looked for again, quietly, on later visits.
- Speed was measured only on 8 test photos on SwiftShader; a large library on the owner's PC was not timed.

**Found in passing (not changed here):** the Sony ILCE-7M2 test file gets the Creator “q” when added, also with main's code (it comes from LibRaw's `imgother.artist`; the file has no EXIF Artist tag in IFD0); and the Part 12 canvas sharpness checks above fail on main.

## Part 7: Lightroom's crop, straighten, orientation, Transform and Upright in sidecars

**What it does.** LightBench now reads and writes Lightroom's own crop, straighten angle, rotate/flip, Upright mode and Transform sliders in `.xmp` sidecars. A crop made in Lightroom appears in LightBench, and a crop changed in LightBench appears in Lightroom. Where the two models differ, LightBench takes the closest match and says so: an Upright mode read from Lightroom is solved again by LightBench's own Upright, and the Transform section says the result may differ slightly from Lightroom's. Nothing renders differently, so the process version stays 1.

Owner's choices (2026-10-12):
1. **Both directions:** read them and write them.
2. **Closest match, said so.** A Lightroom Upright is re-run with LightBench's Upright, with a note in the Transform section.

### The fields and their convention

| Behaviour | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Fields read and written | `crs:HasCrop`, `crs:CropLeft/Top/Right/Bottom`, `crs:CropAngle`, `tiff:Orientation`, `crs:PerspectiveUpright` and `crs:PerspectiveVertical/Horizontal/Rotate/Scale/Aspect/X/Y`. No other `crs:` field is written. | Field names: ExifTool `lib/Image/ExifTool/XMP.pm` (its `crs` table, fetched from ExifTool's GitHub repository); LightCraft `crates/engine/src/crs.rs` lines 416–449 |
| Kept as they are | Lightroom's solved Upright (`crs:UprightVersion`, `UprightCenterMode/NormX/NormY`, `UprightFocalMode/FocalLength35mm`, `UprightPreview`, `UprightTransformCount`, `UprightTransform_0…5`, `UprightFourSegments_0…3/Count`, the digests), `CropConstrainToWarp` and `CropWidth/Height/Units` are neither read nor written. Their conventions aren't documented anywhere available here, and LightCraft doesn't import the matrices either (`crs.rs` `has_saved_upright_transform`, line 514). | LightBench's choice |
| Crop edges | The edges are the crop rectangle's top-left corner (`CropLeft`, `CropTop`) and bottom-right corner (`CropRight`, `CropBottom`) **on the photo as stored**, before `tiff:Orientation` and before straightening, normalised to its width and height. The rectangle is turned by `CropAngle` about the photo's centre, in pixels. LightBench's crop (normalised in the straightened, oriented frame, Part 1) is converted to and from this (`sidecar/lightroom.ts`). | darktable `src/develop/lightroom.c` (commit `6271f53`): reads the edges (lines 492–501), turns both corners about the centre in pixels of `tiff:ImageWidth × ImageLength` (lines 1343–1361), then applies the stored → shown orientation to them (lines 1363–1372). LightCraft `crates/engine/src/lightroom_catalog.rs` `catalog_crop` (line 574): corners “on the stored image (before orientation and straightening)” |
| CropAngle's sign | **LightBench's angle = −CropAngle**, reversed once more for a mirrored orientation (2, 4, 5, 7): angle = −CropAngle × det(orientation). | darktable negates it (`data->pc.angle = -…`, line 501) and negates it again for mirrored orientations (lines 1374–1379). LightCraft's catalog path does the same (“turns the other way”, “a mirror turns the straighten the other way too”). Its comment says it reproduced the frame aspect of all 223 straightened crops of a real catalog in Lightroom's exports within 1 %; a turned crop's aspect depends on the sign, so this checks the sign. A unit test shows darktable's formula and LightCraft's test vectors give LightBench's map |
| LightCraft's inconsistency (review §3.5) | LightCraft's XMP path copies `CropAngle` and the edges unchanged (`crs.rs` line 436), while its catalog path reverses the angle and re-orients the edges. The catalog path was checked against exports, darktable agrees with it, and a catalog and a sidecar hold the same Camera Raw settings. So LightBench follows the catalog path for sidecars. | Resolved from the sources. **Not checked against a sidecar written by Lightroom:** none was available. The immich test assets have no `.xmp` with `crs:Crop`, and LightCraft's crop packets are written for its tests |
| HasCrop | The edges count only when `HasCrop` is True. `HasCrop="False"` means uncropped, angle 0. When LightBench removes a crop it writes `HasCrop="False"` with edges 0, 0, 1, 1 and angle 0. | darktable reads the crop only with HasCrop True (line 514). LightCraft reads False as no crop (`crs.rs` line 448). Adobe's XMP Specification Part 2 describes the edges as valid “when HasCrop is True”; that is from memory, because Adobe's PDF couldn't be fetched here |
| tiff:Orientation | Lightroom keeps its Rotate and Flip in the photo's orientation (EXIF codes 1–8), not in the crop. LightBench's turns and flips sit on top of the camera's orientation, so they are read as the sidecar's orientation × the inverse of the file's own, and written back as the total. A sidecar without `tiff:Orientation` shows the file's own orientation. The crop is stored on the unrotated photo, so turning a photo changes only `tiff:Orientation` (unit test). | darktable (`_lrop` “Orientation”, line 506; the flip history step, lines 1388–1393); the EXIF orientation codes |
| CropConstrainToWarp | ExifTool lists it among the fields Lightroom 3 added with its manual lens-correction sliders (PerspectiveVertical, Horizontal, Rotate, Scale). So it is most likely Lightroom's **Constrain Crop** checkbox: crop to the warped (lens-corrected, transformed) photo so no empty areas show. LightCraft treats it as a tool option, not an adjustment (`crs.rs` line 91). LightBench always constrains (Part 3), so it neither reads nor writes it. | ExifTool's grouping (“new tags written by LR 3”). The meaning is inferred, not documented |
| crs:AlreadyApplied | True means the pixels already contain these settings, as in an exported file. LightBench reads no geometry from such a packet and writes none into it. | ExifTool (“written by LightRoom beta 4.1”); LightCraft skips such packets (`crs.rs` line 100) |
| Upright modes | `PerspectiveUpright` 0 Off, 1 Auto, **2 Full, 3 Level, 4 Vertical**, 5 Guided. | ExifTool (`XMP.pm`, PerspectiveUpright, citing its forum thread 14012, each code annotated with Lightroom's tooltip). LightCraft's table says 2 Level, 3 Vertical, 4 Full (`docs/xmp-interop.md`; `crs.rs` line 423) and doesn't say how it was found. ExifTool's comes from observing Lightroom, so LightBench uses it. **Not checked in Lightroom** |
| Transform sliders | They map number for number (both −100…100; Rotate ±10°; Scale in %; the offsets). In Lightroom, Vertical − widens the top, Horizontal − enlarges the left side, Rotate + turns clockwise, Aspect + stretches vertically, X + moves right and Y + moves up. So LightBench writes its Vertical, Horizontal and Aspect negated, and Rotate, Scale and the offsets unchanged. A Lightroom Scale below 100 % reads as 100 %, because LightBench never shows empty edges. A step's strength differs (darktable's lens shift isn't Lightroom's keystone), so the numbers carry over but the look only approximately. | LightCraft's description of Lightroom (`crates/pipeline/src/transform.rs` `manual`, “like Lightroom”). **Not checked in Lightroom** |
| Lens correction and Transform | LightBench's crop is in the lens-corrected, transformed frame (Part 1) and is written as Lightroom's edges unchanged. With lens correction or a transform the two frames aren't the same picture, so the crop lands on approximately the same content. | Closest match |

### Reading

| Behaviour | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| New photos | Open folder or Import may add a photo whose sidecar (or a JPEG's embedded XMP) has Lightroom geometry that would change it. The background developer (the one Paste uses) then applies that geometry as a history step **“Lightroom crop & Transform”**, solves a Lightroom Upright mode again on the photo and makes the thumbnail. Several such photos form one job. The preset for new photos still applies to them. A sidecar that holds LightBench's own edit keeps that edit, so edits saved before this part read as before. The exception is when Lightroom changed its fields after LightBench last wrote them (see Writing). | Owner's choice 2; Paste's re-run (`develop/uprightRun.ts` `rerunUpright`, `develop/batch.ts`) |
| Only what differs | Each group (orientation, crop, Transform) is taken only when it differs from LightBench's. So what Lightroom can't describe stays: the aspect lock, a Guided correction drawn in LightBench, or an Upright LightBench already solved for the same mode. A crop read from Lightroom is left unlocked, so it keeps Lightroom's exact shape; the aspect menu shows the nearest named shape. | LightBench |
| Upright | For Auto, Level, Vertical and Full, the mode is set and solved again by LightBench's Upright on the photo. Lightroom keeps a straighten angle and a crop together with Upright, but LightBench's Upright resets the angle, so the crop and angle read are put back after solving. The Transform section shows: “Upright Vertical came from Lightroom's settings. LightBench ran its own Upright, so the result may differ slightly from Lightroom's.” The note goes away when another mode is chosen. | Owner's choice 2 |
| Guided | Lightroom's guide lines (`UprightFourSegments_*`) can't be read. The mode is set without a correction, and the note says: “Lightroom's Guided Upright wasn't imported: its guide lines can't be read. Draw guides here to correct the photo.” | Owner's requirement |
| Changed by Lightroom | The fields LightBench compares to notice another app's change (Phase 2, “Sidecars changed by other apps”) now include Lightroom's geometry, resolved the way Lightroom shows it. A crop changed in Lightroom raises the usual notice (whose text now mentions Lightroom's crop and Transform). **Read changes** applies the new geometry through the background developer (history step “Read from sidecar”) and solves an Upright mode again. A record made before this part has no geometry; the file's geometry is then taken as the base, so a Lightroom crop LightBench didn't read before isn't reported as a new change. Changes to Lightroom fields LightBench doesn't use (e.g. `UprightVersion`, `CropConstrainToWarp`) still don't count. | Phase 2's flow |

### Writing

| Behaviour | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Only into Camera Raw sidecars | The fields are written only into a sidecar that already has Lightroom or Camera Raw settings (`crs:ProcessVersion`), and never into an already-applied one. A sidecar with a few `crs:` fields and no process version might make Lightroom treat the photo as having settings of an old process: settings files from before process versions existed carry none. That wasn't checked, and LightBench shouldn't choose Lightroom's process version. So a photo only ever edited in LightBench gets no Lightroom fields. The owner chose to keep this rule until it can be tested in Lightroom (2026-10-13). | LightBench's caution |
| Only what LightBench changed | A group is written only when LightBench's geometry differs from the fields **and** LightBench changed it since it last wrote the file, that is, when the file's own `lightbench:Develop` shows other geometry (or none, when the file has none yet). So opening or importing a Lightroom-cropped photo never rewrites the crop, even before LightBench has read it or when reading it failed. An edit made before this part, when LightBench didn't read Lightroom's crop, leaves Lightroom's fields alone until its geometry is changed in LightBench. | Owner's requirement |
| After Ignore | LightBench stores `lightbench:LightroomGeometry`, the Lightroom geometry it was last in step with. If the fields no longer match it (Lightroom changed them and the user chose **Ignore**), LightBench's geometry replaces them on the next write, as Ignore promises for other fields. The same marker lets a new library (a re-import) tell Lightroom's later changes from LightBench's own. | LightBench |
| In place | A changed field keeps its place: its form (attribute or element) and the order of attributes. A missing field is added next to the description's other `crs:` fields. Every other field and namespace keeps its exact name, value and content. As before this part, LightBench's writer (the browser's XMLSerializer) puts a start tag's attributes on one line and drops the whitespace around the packet wrapper; names, values and element content don't change (e2e below). | Phase 2's merge |
| Format | Edges with six decimals (as Lightroom writes them), the angle to 1e-6°, Vertical/Horizontal/Scale/Aspect as integers (ExifTool types them as integers), Rotate and the offsets with one decimal. | ExifTool's types |

### Copy, paste and old edits

| Behaviour | Detail |
|---|---|
| Copy/Paste checklist | Unchanged. The note's marker (`transform.lightroom`, which renders nothing) isn't copied: pasting Upright, choosing a mode or drawing Guided lines drops it. The paste tests pass unchanged. |
| Old edits | An edit without the marker reads exactly as before (`normalizeTransform` adds no key). An old LightBench sidecar without `crs:` geometry reads the same, and a LightBench edit next to Lightroom fields is kept. No catalog schema change: the comparison base lives in the existing sidecar record's JSON. |

### Also changed

| Behaviour | Detail |
|---|---|
| Background developer after a lost GPU device | When the GPU device is lost on one photo (a driver reset; in the e2e runs the software GPU lost it while Develop and the background developer both held full-size photos), the background developer now starts a new engine for the next photo instead of failing every later photo with “The graphics device was lost”. Found by the e2e below; it affects Paste and Sync the same way. |

**Verified** (headless Chromium, software WebGPU (SwiftShader), on the code merged with main at 0.4.1; A.NEF = Nikon D700 `philadelphia.nef`, 4284 × 2844; K.JPG and G.JPG = Part 3's synthetic keystone, a facade shot 12° up at 36 mm; scratch script `e2e-lrcrop`, 19 of 19 checks passed):
- **A Lightroom crop and angle are read** (`A.xmp` laid out as Lightroom Classic writes sidecars, written for the test: `crs:ProcessVersion` 15.4, CropTop/Left/Bottom/Right 0.0815/0.1023/0.9021/0.8817, CropAngle 2.75, HasCrop True, Transform and solved-Upright fields, a tone curve, `xmpMM:History`, keywords and the packet wrapper). Opening the folder applied it as one step “Lightroom crop & Transform”: angle −2.75°, crop equal to darktable's reading of the same fields (computed in the script from `lightroom.c`'s formulas, independently of LightBench's code) to better than 1e-5. **Visual match:** A2.NEF, the same RAW with darktable's reading given as LightBench's own settings, showed the same crop (3446 × 2170) and an identical Develop canvas (mean difference 0.000 levels, max 0).
- **Reading doesn't rewrite Lightroom's fields:** after the import (which saved LightBench's settings into `A.xmp`), all 43 of Lightroom's attributes were unchanged byte for byte, the tone curve and history unchanged, the packet wrapper kept.
- **Upright Vertical from a sidecar** (K.xmp, the JPEG's own `NAME.xmp` as it owns its base name: `crs:PerspectiveUpright="4"`): LightBench set Vertical and solved it again (vertical lens shift 0.1358, as Part 3's Vertical gave on this photo), the Vertical button was on, the Transform section showed the note (“Upright Vertical came from Lightroom's settings. LightBench ran its own Upright, so the result may differ slightly from Lightroom's.”), and the verticals measured 0.01° from vertical (2.58° before Upright, Part 3). G.xmp with Guided (code 5) and two `UprightFourSegments`: “Lightroom's Guided Upright wasn't imported: its guide lines can't be read. Draw guides here to correct the photo.” Screenshot of the Transform section with the note checked by eye.
- **A crop changed in LightBench is written:** the Angle slider moved two steps (−2.75° → −1.85°). `A.xmp` then had CropAngle 1.85 and edges equal (to 2e-6) to Lightroom's edges for LightBench's crop computed by inverting darktable's reading; the crop fields kept their places in the attribute order; the other 37 attributes (Camera Raw, TIFF, XMP, xmpMM) and the two elements LightBench doesn't own were unchanged (names, values and content; the start tags' attribute layout was put on one line, as LightBench's writer always does); the Transform and solved-Upright fields weren't touched.
- **An outside change is detected and Read changes applies it:** the script changed `A.xmp`'s crop as Lightroom would (edges 0.2/0.15/0.75/0.85, CropAngle −1.2). Showing the photo again raised “1 photo was changed by another app …”; **Read changes** applied the new crop (equal to darktable's reading to 1e-5, angle +1.2°) as the history step “Read from sidecar”, and left Lightroom's fields as Lightroom wrote them.
- **Unit tests** (`npx vitest run`, 527 passing; 27 new in `sidecar/lightroom.test.ts`): darktable's formula and LightCraft's own catalog test vectors (every orientation, the mirrored angle) give LightBench's map; LightBench → Lightroom → LightBench round-trips for the four camera orientations × four turns × mirror × four angles; turning or flipping changes only `tiff:Orientation`; the EXIF codes; HasCrop; a Lightroom crop renders inside the photo; the Upright codes and slider signs both ways; Scale under 100 %; re-solve and the notes; LightBench's own solve kept for the same mode; old edits get no new keys; reading the fields; opening a cropped photo writes nothing; a changed crop written in place with every other attribute and element byte-identical; element-form fields updated in place; orientation and Transform written only when changed, Lightroom's solved Upright untouched; nothing written without `crs:ProcessVersion` or with AlreadyApplied; an older edit kept until changed; the marker after Ignore; Lightroom's crop change reported and merged; old records don't report a crop LightBench didn't read; Lightroom fields LightBench doesn't use don't count. A mutation check (CropAngle's sign flipped) failed 6 of them. The existing sidecar, change-detection and paste tests pass unchanged. `npx tsc -b`, `npx oxlint` (the two existing warnings) and `npm run build` passed.

**Limitations and unverified:**
- **Nothing was checked in Lightroom itself**, and no sidecar written by Lightroom was available. The crop convention rests on darktable's importer and LightCraft's catalog importer (checked by its authors against 223 exported crops); the Upright codes on ExifTool (LightCraft's differing table is not used); the slider signs on LightCraft's description of Lightroom. How Lightroom treats a mode LightBench wrote while Lightroom's own `UprightTransform_*` matrices are still in the file (re-analysis, or reuse of the matrix for that mode) is unknown.
- LightBench writes Lightroom's fields only into sidecars that already hold Camera Raw settings, so a photo edited only in LightBench shows no crop in Lightroom. Writing into other sidecars would need checking how Lightroom treats `crs:` fields without `crs:ProcessVersion`.
- Lightroom's Upright is solved again by LightBench (darktable's model), so the correction differs somewhat; Guided lines aren't imported; the Transform sliders' strength differs per step.
- With lens correction or a transform, LightBench's crop frame (lens-corrected and fitted, Part 1 and Part 3) and Lightroom's aren't the same picture, so the crop lands on approximately the same content.
- `CropConstrainToWarp`'s meaning (Constrain Crop) is inferred from ExifTool's grouping; `CropWidth/Height/Units` aren't touched.
- The stored size comes from the catalog. For a JPEG whose EXIF has an orientation but no pixel dimensions, the catalog may hold the turned size, which would turn a straightened Lightroom crop slightly wrongly; JPEGs with mirrored EXIF orientations are read as unmirrored, as before.
- As before this part, LightBench's sidecar writer re-serializes the file: names, values and element content are kept, but whitespace inside start tags and around the packet wrapper isn't.
- A photo whose Lightroom geometry was read but whose edit wasn't written yet (the folder waiting for write permission), and whose crop is then removed in LightBench, keeps Lightroom's crop in the sidecar until the crop is changed again.

## Part 8: Output sharpening on Export

**What it does.** The Export dialog has an **Output sharpening** section, as Lightroom Classic's Export dialog does (owner, 2026-10-12): **Sharpen for** Screen, Matte paper or Glossy paper, with an **Amount** of Low, Standard or High. It is off by default. Only the exported file is sharpened, as the last step after it has been resized to its final size; the photo's edit, Develop, the 100 % view and thumbnails don't change, so no process version is involved. The strength follows the export: for paper it is sized by the print resolution (a new **Resolution** field under Size, written into the file), for screen by how much the photo is downsized. The choice is remembered with the other export settings (this browser's `lightbench.export`, which backups already include). LightBench has no export presets yet; when they come, they store the whole `ExportSettings` and so this choice too.

**What it is not.** Lightroom's output sharpening comes from PixelGenius PhotoKit Sharpener and isn't published. LightBench doesn't try to match it, and the numbers below are not Lightroom's. Adobe's help says only that its output sharpening is adaptive, “based on the output media and resolution you choose”, that it adds to the sharpening done in Develop, and that Standard suits most cases (Adobe, *Export files to disk or CD*, Lightroom Classic help, read through a search summary; the page itself couldn't be opened from the test machine).

### The algorithm

| Behaviour | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Sharpening | darktable's unsharp mask, with its maths unchanged: a separable Gaussian blur of Lab L with a window of `ceil(2.5 · sigma)` pixels each side (at most 12), detail = L − blurred L, a **soft threshold** (differences below the threshold are left alone and the threshold is subtracted from larger ones), L′ = L + amount · detail, a and b kept. darktable's “radius” slider is the Gaussian's sigma: `commit_params` multiplies it by 2.5 for the window and the kernel divides by 2.5 again. | darktable `src/iop/sharpen.c`: parameters and defaults (radius 2, amount 0.5, threshold 0.5) lines 44-49, Lab input 84-89, kernel 112-124, window `rad` 258, sigma 282-283, threshold and mix 353-361, `commit_params` 376-379 |
| Luminance only | L* changes and a* and b* are kept, so fx, fy and fz all move by ΔL/116 and the pixel changes by the matching XYZ difference (`export.wgsl` `sharpenMixEncode`). Lab's white is the working space's white (linear Rec.2020, D65), so greys stay grey. Pixels the threshold leaves alone pass through untouched. | darktable (Lab L only) |
| Where | In `renderOutput` (`engine/gpu/develop-engine.ts`, `export.wgsl`): develop at full size → Lanczos3 resize in linear light → **sharpen** → output colour space and tone curve → quantise. The image is display-referred at this point (after the tone mapping), and L* is a perceptual scale, as in darktable's Lab sharpen. Full-size exports are sharpened straight from the developed image. | LightBench |
| Why after the resize | darktable has no output sharpening. Its sharpen module sits early in the pipe (`src/common/iop_order.c`, v5.0 order: sharpen at 35.0, line 366; finalscale 69.4 and colorout 70.0, lines 417-418). Without “high quality processing” an export runs the whole pipe at the output scale and sharpen's sigma is multiplied by that scale (`roi_in->scale / piece->iscale`, sharpen.c 258 and 282), so the sharpening stays relative to the full-size photo; with it, the pipe runs at full size and `finalscale` downsizes near the end, after sharpen (`src/imageio/imageio.c`, `dt_imageio_export_with_flags`, lines 1279 and 1338-1381; `src/iop/finalscale.c` 100-103). Either way darktable sharpens before the resize. Output sharpening is meant for the final pixels, so LightBench runs darktable's filter on the resized image, with sigma in output pixels (scale 1). | darktable, as cited |
| Edges of the photo | Samples beyond the border repeat the edge pixel, so the border is sharpened too. darktable leaves the outer `rad` pixels unsharpened instead (sharpen.c 296-305, 341-342, 362-363). | LightBench |
| Halo limit | The sharpened L* is kept within the lightest and darkest L* of the pixel's 3 × 3 neighbourhood, widened by **10 %** of their difference. darktable's sharpen has no such limit. It was added because a 25 % limit still let a perfectly sharp mid-grey edge overshoot by 36 levels at the stronger settings (below); on soft, photographic edges it changed no edge width. L* is also kept within 0–100 unless the pixel was already outside. | LightBench, chosen by eye on the test chart |
| Off | Without sharpening the export runs the same shader entry points as before (`encodeOnly`, `resampleX`, `resampleYEncode`; the vertical pass only moved into a helper) and is byte-identical to 0.4.1 (below). | — |

### The parameter table (LightBench's own)

Chosen by LightBench and checked by eye on the test chart and two photos; **not Lightroom's values and not checked on prints**. Amount and threshold are in darktable's units (amount multiplies the detail; threshold is in L*, 0–100). `export/sharpen.ts`, `SHARPEN_TABLE`.

| Sharpen for | Radius (Gaussian sigma) | Low | Standard | High | Threshold |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Screen | 0.78 px at 100 % size, shrinking towards 0.5 px as the export is downsized: √(0.5² + (0.6 · scale)²) | 0.35 | 0.6 | 0.9 | 0.5 |
| Glossy paper | 0.075 mm on the print, × ppi / 25.4: 0.71 px at 240 ppi, **0.89 px at 300 ppi**, 1.77 px at 600 ppi | 0.5 | 0.85 | 1.25 | 0.5 |
| Matte paper | 0.095 mm on the print: 0.90 px at 240 ppi, **1.12 px at 300 ppi**, 2.24 px at 600 ppi | 0.65 | 1.1 | 1.6 | 0.5 |

- **Radius limits:** sigma is held between 0.25 px and 4.8 px (darktable's 12-pixel window); the window is `ceil(2.5 · sigma)`.
- **Screen:** an export is usually seen at one pixel per screen pixel, so the radius is in output pixels. The softness a photo has at 100 % (lens, demosaic) shrinks when it is downsized, so the radius does too: 0.78 px at full size, 0.58 px at half size, 0.52 px at a quarter (`screenSigma`; “scale” is output pixels / photo pixels; enlargements count as 100 %). The 0.6 px for that softness is LightBench's assumption, not measured on cameras.
- **Paper:** the radius is a physical size on the print, converted with the Resolution, so a 600 ppi print gets twice the pixel radius of a 300 ppi one and the same look on paper. Matte paper gets a larger radius and amount than glossy, and screen the least. That order follows common print practice (uncoated matte papers spread ink more and show less contrast; general knowledge, not checked against a source), and users testing Lightroom report the same order (Screen < Glossy < Matte, in a dgrin.com forum thread seen through a search summary: **anecdotal, not an Adobe statement**). The millimetre values are LightBench's.
- **Threshold 0.5** is darktable's default. **Resolution** defaults to 300 ppi, the value LightBench always wrote into its files; it accepts 72–1200 ppi. Adobe's help suggests 180–480 ppi for inkjet prints (search summary, as above).
- Under the buttons the dialog says what the first photo gets, e.g. “Radius 0.71 px, amount 1.25 for glossy paper at 240 ppi (a 21.7 × 14.4 cm print)”.

### The dialog

| Control | Detail |
|---|---|
| Output sharpening | A checkbox “Sharpen for”, with a one-line note that only the exported file is sharpened. When it is on: three buttons (Screen, Matte paper, Glossy paper) and three for Amount (Low, Standard, High), the same chip buttons as Format, each with a tooltip saying what it is for, and the note on the first photo. Turning it on first gives Screen / Standard. |
| Resolution | Under Size: “Resolution [300] pixels per inch”, with a tooltip: it is written into the file, a print's size is its pixels divided by it, and paper sharpening is sized for it. Values outside 72–1200 are corrected when the field is left. It is written wherever LightBench already wrote 300 ppi: PNG always, JPEG and TIFF with the camera metadata. |

### Verified

Headless Chromium, software WebGPU (SwiftShader); scratch scripts in `scratchpad/followups-c/` (`e2e-sharpen.mjs`, `mkchart.py`, `analyse.py`, `photo-crops.py`). Test files: the Nikon D700 RAW `philadelphia.nef` (immich test-assets), `test-srgb.jpg`, and a synthetic 2400 × 1600 chart (q100 4:4:4 JPEG): slanted edges at 5° (64/192 and 96/160 grey, red/cyan and green/yellow, blurred by a Gaussian of sigma 1 px like a photo; 64/192 and black/white perfectly sharp), flat colour patches, a blurred noise texture and fine bars.

- **Off is byte-identical to main (0.4.1, `6b69136`).** Both were built (`vite build`), served with `vite preview` and driven through the Export dialog with a fixed clock (the export time goes into EXIF and the ICC profile): the RAW and the JPEG, as JPEG and 8-bit PNG at full size, JPEG at 1024 px long edge and 16-bit PNG at 1024 px, from stored settings without the new keys (as main leaves them). All 8 files had the same SHA-256 (e.g. the RAW's full-size JPEG `b69f30b9ad195254…`, its 1024 px 16-bit PNG `191cff85235ed5fd…`).
- **Sharper edges** (chart, full size, 8-bit PNG without dither; edge spread function of each slanted edge in ⅛ px bins; “10-90” is the 10–90 % rise distance, “slope” the steepest rise in levels per pixel, “over/under” the overshoot past the bright and dark plateaus in 8-bit levels):

  | Export | 64/192 soft edge: 10-90; slope change; over / under | 96/160 soft edge: 10-90 | 64/192 sharp edge: over / under |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | Off | 2.65 px; —; 0.0 / 0.1 | 2.65 px | 0.1 / 0.1 |
  | Screen Low / Standard / High | 2.52 / 2.46 / 2.36 px; +11 / +13 / +19 %; at most 0.4 / 1.0 | 2.52 / 2.51 / 2.41 px | 11.3 / 8.7, then 14.2 / 11.3 |
  | Glossy Low / Standard / High (300 ppi) | 2.44 / 2.30 / 2.15 px; +12 / +20 / +32 %; at most 1.7 / 1.4 | 2.49 / 2.37 / 2.23 px | 14.2 / 11.3 |
  | Matte Low / Standard / High (300 ppi) | 2.25 / 2.02 / 1.77 px; +23 / +37 / +54 %; at most 4.0 / 3.6 | 2.29 / 2.08 / 1.87 px | 14.2 / 11.3 |
  | Matte High, 600 ppi | 1.17 px; +96 %; 4.0 / 3.9 | 1.23 px | 14.2 / 11.3 |

  The soft edge narrows by 5–11 % for screen, 8–19 % for glossy and 15–33 % for matte at 300 ppi. Downsized to 50 % (Screen / Standard, sigma 0.58 px): 1.43 → 1.22 px.
- **Haloes:** with the 10 % limit the perfectly sharp 64/192 edge overshoots by at most 14.2 levels above and 11.3 below, a one-pixel line; with a 25 % limit (first run) it reached 35.6 / 28.0, while the soft edges' widths were the same. The black/white edge can't overshoot (0 above; 1 level below, where pixels next to the edge go from 1 to 0). On a 50 % downsized sharp edge the Lanczos3 resize itself already undershoots by 31.8 levels without sharpening; Screen / Standard makes that 42.3 (the limit allows 10 % past the neighbourhood, which includes that ringing).
- **No colour shift:** the chart's mean colour changed by at most +0.13 level per channel (8-bit; +0.18 at Matte High, 600 ppi), and by +0.125 / +0.120 / +0.111 level measured in 16-bit at Matte High; flat patches 0; the noise texture's mean at most 0.03 level. Across the soft red/cyan edge the chromaticity r/(r+g+b) moved by at most 0.03 at the edge pixels (mean 0.0001), and no colour fringe was visible at 400 %.
- **Real photos** (Screen / Standard at full size, against off): the D700 RAW's mean colour moved +0.033 / +0.035 / +0.034 level and `test-srgb.jpg`'s +0.023 / +0.019 / +0.014; local contrast (mean |Laplacian| of luma) rose 17 % and 22 %; 22–24 % of the pixels changed (the rest stay below the threshold). Crops at 100 % and 200 % of the most detailed areas (water spray, foliage) looked crisper without haloes or colour fringes (checked by eye).
- **The dialog** (`e2e-sharpen.mjs photo`): off by default with no buttons shown; turned on with its checkbox, Glossy paper and High clicked, Resolution typed as 240: the note read “Radius 0.71 px, amount 1.25 for glossy paper at 240 ppi (a 21.7 × 14.4 cm print)”; both photos exported (2048 px JPEG); `lightbench.export` held `sharpen: {on: true, medium: 'glossy', amount: 'high'}` and `ppi: 240`, and the reopened dialog showed them. Neither photo had a saved edit before or after the exports. Screenshots of the dialog were checked by eye.
- **Unit tests** (`export/sharpen.test.ts`, 9): off by default and 300 ppi in the defaults; the table's values; amounts rise Low < Standard < High, and matte > glossy > screen in amount and in amount × radius; darktable's window `ceil(2.5 · sigma)` ≤ 12 and the sigma limits over 72–1200 ppi and scales 0.05–2; screen sigma 0.78 / 0.58 / 0.52 / 0.5 px at scales 1 / ½ / ¼ / 0, enlargements as 100 %, independent of the resolution; paper sigma proportional to ppi and independent of the downsizing; ppi clamping; unknown stored values fall back to Screen / Standard; the dialog's note. `npx tsc -b`, `npx oxlint` (the two existing warnings), `npx vitest run` and `npm run build` passed.

**Limitations and unverified:**
- **Nothing was printed.** The paper settings were judged on screen, at 100 % and 400 %; whether Matte and Glossy at each amount look right on real paper is untested, and the table may need adjusting after test prints.
- **Not compared with Lightroom**, which wasn't available: how these strengths compare with Lightroom's Low / Standard / High is unknown.
